Hi, Doctor. Thank you so much for fitting me in, I really appreciate it. Um... I hope I'm in the right place, and I don't want to make a huge fuss or anything, but... I guess what brought me in is I've been having this issue at work. I do maintenance and repair, so it's a pretty loud job, you know? 

But lately... well, I just can't stop jumping out of my skin. Like, if a breaker snaps, or somebody drops a heavy tool, or even if one of the guys just comes up behind me... I just overreact. It's a little embarrassing, honestly. My heart just starts hammering in my chest, and it just keeps pounding long after everyone else has moved on and forgotten about it. I'm trying my best to keep it together and do a good job, I promise. Does... does that sound like the kind of thing you can help with? I hope I'm explaining it right.

Um... well, I guess... I mean, I try not to let it affect my work, I really do. But... yeah, I suppose I have been kind of steering clear of a few things. 

Like, I'll make up an excuse to not work on certain panels or breakers, or I'll try to swap tasks if it's a specific job site. And... I really don't like talking about that day, either. If the guys bring it up, I just kind of walk away or change the subject. It's just... once those thoughts start up in my head, I can't shut them off, you know? It's easier to just avoid it all so I don't cause a scene or look like I can't handle my job. 

I'm sorry, I know that probably sounds bad, like I'm not doing my fair share at work. I'm really trying to get better about it, I promise. Is... is it normal to want to avoid things like that? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.

Um... well, I guess... yeah, sometimes. I mean, I really try not to dwell on it, I want to just move past it and do my job, you know? But... sometimes, out of nowhere, I'll just see this huge flash in my head. Or... or I'll hear the trainee yelling.

Um... well, it's... it's the accident, mostly. The one with the trainee. I really try not to think about it because... well, I don't want to make it a bigger deal than it is, and I know I need to just focus on moving forward.

I hope I'm explaining it right.\n\nDoctor: Are these overreactions happening only at work or also in other situations?\n\nPatient: Um... well, I guess I haven't really noticed it as much outside of work? I mean, the job site is where most of the loud noises and heavy equipment are, so that's where it really stands out. I guess maybe I get a little jumpy at home if a door slams unexpectedly or something, but it's really nothing major. \n\nI try really hard to just leave it at work, you know? I don't want to be a bother to anyone at home with my weird reactions, so I just try to brush it off. I'm probably just making a bigger deal out of it than it really is. Does... does that make sense? I hope I'm answering your question right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge in quiet or everyday situations, like when you're watching TV or out with friends?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really. I mean, when I'm just sitting at home watching TV, or if I'm out grabbing a beer with the guys, I feel fine. I try to be pretty laid back outside of work. \n\nI guess I don't really feel on edge unless there's, you know, a reason to be. I really try to just enjoy my downtime and not let things get to me. I'm usually pretty good at just relaxing when things are quiet. I hope that's a good thing? I mean, I try not to let it affect my work, I really do. It's just... once those thoughts start up in my head, I can't shut them off, you know? I'm really trying to get better about it, I promise. Is... is it normal to want to avoid things like that? Um... well, I really try hard to keep everything running smooth, you know? I don't want to be a burden on anyone or let anybody down. But... I guess if I'm being completely honest—and I'm really embarrassed to admit this, so I hope you don't think less of me—it has been causing some problems lately. 

I actually... I got written up at work. And they ended up pulling me out of the running for a supervisor spot I was up for. I feel terrible about it, I really wanted to do a good job for them. And at home... well, my wife says I'm just barely present anymore. She told me it feels like everything in my life has started revolving around just... avoiding another blowup like that day. 

I hate that I'm putting her through that, and I really want to fix it and do better. I hope it's okay that I'm telling you all this... I know it sounds like I'm failing at everything right now, but I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information so I can get back to being a good husband and a good worker. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Doctor. Thank you so much for fitting me in, I really appreciate it. Um... I hope I'm in the right place, and I don't want to make a huge fuss or anything, but... I guess what brought me in is I've been having this issue at work. I do maintenance and repair, so it's a pretty loud job, you know? \n\nBut lately... well, I just can't stop jumping out of my skin. Like, if a breaker snaps, or somebody drops a heavy tool, or even if one of the guys just comes up behind me... I just overreact. It's a little embarrassing, honestly. My heart just starts hammering in my chest, and it just keeps pounding long after everyone else has moved on and forgotten about it. I'm trying my best to keep it together and do a good job, I promise. Does... does that sound like the kind of thing you can help with? I hope I'm explaining it right.\n\nDoctor: Are these overreactions happening only at work or also in other situations?\n\nPatient: Um... well, I guess I haven't really noticed it as much outside of work? I mean, the job site is where most of the loud noises and heavy equipment are, so that's where it really stands out. I guess maybe I get a little jumpy at home if a door slams unexpectedly or something, but it's really nothing major. \n\nI try really hard to just leave it at work, you know? I don't want to be a bother to anyone at home with my weird reactions, so I just try to brush it off. I'm probably just making a bigger deal out of it than it really is. Does... does that make sense? I hope I'm answering your question right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge in quiet or everyday situations, like when you're watching TV or out with friends?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really. I try to be pretty laid back outside of work. \n\nI guess I don't really feel on edge unless there's, you know, a reason to be. I really try to just enjoy my downtime and not let things get to me. It's just... once those thoughts start up in my head, I can't shut them off, you know? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving a particularly frightening or traumatic moment from your past?\n\nPatient: Um... well, I guess... yeah, sometimes. I mean, I really try not to dwell on it, I want to just move past it and do my job, you know? But... sometimes, out of nowhere, I'll just see this huge flash in my head. Or... or I'll hear the trainee yelling. \n\nIt happens even when I'm doing something completely normal, like just loading my tools into the back of the truck. It just kind of knocks me sideways for a second, like I'm right back there and it's happening all over again. \n\nI try to shake it off really quick so nobody notices. I don't want the guys thinking I'm losing my mind or that I'm not safe to be around on the site. I'm sorry, I know I shouldn't be letting it get to me like that. I'm really trying to keep it under control. Is... is it okay that I'm telling you this? I just want to make sure I'm answering what you're asking.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that happened on the job that you're trying to avoid thinking about or remembering?\n\nPatient: Um... well, it's... it's the accident, mostly. The one with the trainee. I really try not to think about it because... well, I don't want to make it a bigger deal than it is, and I know I need to just focus on moving forward. \n\nBut it's weird... I can remember pieces of it clear as day, like the smell of the ozone and that huge flash of light. But... the exact few seconds right before he got hurt? Like what I actually did, or what I said to him right then? It's just... blank. Or kind of mixed up in my head. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that sounds like I wasn't paying attention or that I'm trying to hide something, but I swear I'm not. I just... I really can't remember that specific part. I hope that's okay to admit? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right details, even if it makes me look a little bad.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of the accident with the trainee?\n\nPatient: Um... yeah, I guess I do. I know I mentioned it a little bit earlier, but... well, I just try to make up excuses so I don't have to go near certain panels or breakers. And if we're scheduled for a specific job site that feels too much like... like that day, I'll try to swap tasks with one of the other guys. \n\nI really try not to let it be a burden on anyone, I swear. But... I also just can't stand talking about it. If someone brings up the accident, I just have to walk away. It's just... once those thoughts start spinning in my head, I really can't shut them off. So it's just easier to avoid it all, you know? \n\nI'm sorry, I know I'm repeating myself a bit... I just want to make sure I'm answering you right. Is it okay that I'm doing that? I'm really trying to handle it the best I can without causing any trouble at work.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these overreactions and flashbacks?\n\nPatient: Um... well, it's... it's been a little while now. I mean, I really thought it would just go away on its own if I gave it some time, you know? But... it's been happening ever since the arc-flash accident on that retrofit job. So... about a year and a month now. \n\nI know that sounds like a long time to let this go on without saying anything, and I'm really sorry I didn't get help sooner. I just kept thinking I could tough it out and not make a fuss. I hope you're not disappointed that I waited so long to come in. I really am trying to do the right thing now... is that... is that helpful for you to know?\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific memories or thoughts about the accident that you try to push away or avoid thinking about?\n\nPatient: Um... I guess... well, no, not really anything specific. I mean, I know I just said I don't like talking about it, and I do try to just... keep my mind on the job. But it's not like there's one particular thought I'm constantly fighting off, if that makes sense? \n\nI just try to focus on the positive and keep moving forward, so I don't really dwell on it enough to have specific thoughts I'm pushing away. I hope that makes sense? I'm sorry if that's not the answer you were looking for... I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture of how things are. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: Are there any people that you avoid because they remind you of the accident with the trainee?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really. I mean, the guys I work with are all great, and I try to get along with everyone. I wouldn't want anyone to feel like I'm avoiding them or, you know, holding anything against them. It wasn't anyone's fault, so I definitely don't avoid any of the guys on the crew. \n\nI just try to be a good team player and work with whoever I'm assigned to. I hope that's a good thing, right? I really don't want to cause any friction at work, so I make sure to treat everyone the same as I always did. Is that... is that the right way to handle it?\n\nDoctor: Has your reaction to the accident and the subsequent avoidance of certain situations or tasks significantly impacted your ability to perform your job or affected your relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Um... well, I really try hard to keep everything running smooth, you know? I don't want to be a burden on anyone or let anybody down. But... I guess if I'm being completely honest—and I'm really embarrassed to admit this, so I hope you don't think less of me—it has been causing some problems lately. \n\nI actually... I got written up at work. And they ended up pulling me out of the running for a supervisor spot I was up for. I feel terrible about it, I really wanted to do a good job for them. And at home... well, my wife says I'm just barely present anymore. She told me it feels like everything in my life has started revolving around just... avoiding another blowup like that day. \n\nI hate that I'm putting her through that, and I really want to fix it and do better. I hope it's okay that I'm telling you all this... I know it sounds like I'm failing at everything right now, but I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information so I can get back to being a good husband and a good worker. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um... well, I mean, I don't want you to think I'm just running away from my problems or anything. I really do try to be a hard worker and face things head-on. But... yeah, I guess I do try not to think about it. 

I just... I make up excuses, you know? Like, at work, I'll find a reason not to go near certain panels or breakers, or I'll ask to be put on a different job site. I hope that doesn't make me sound weak or like I'm not trying to get better. I just... I want to keep doing a good job at work and not let it interfere, so it just seems easier to steer clear of those things. Is that... is that an okay way to handle it for now? I just want to make sure I'm doing what I'm supposed to be doing.
